the Door-Keepers Ancestor-meals set a place for the dead who stayed in the ground. A traveller can mistake that place for the extra seat. The dead in the ground are not the hands that fetch and pour. The isle is not a grove.
the Shore-Sitters Sister house. They sit the storm-beach. A living wreck they walk uphill; they do not seat on the sand. A Table-Keeper does not go down to wait. The sand decides alive; the hall decides kin.

How they live in the three corners (and off the map)

Vaethorn, Lestrand, and Threnmaieth barely reach this house.

  • Kumbaan (hill-hosts). This is their home. The Njaalo is simply who opened the hall. The moon sets the night, not the solstice. Wholeness is the default. They keep no queue, no roll, no book, and no second reading.
  • Lestrand (the Moon-Feast people). A quay-story. An attic-host is a curiosity and a good supper. “No one is a stranger twice” is printed, by sailors, as a proverb on a card. The Settling will not enforce it. The attic-host will, inside the room, and cannot outside it.
  • Vaethorn (a sailor’s tale). Hot faith, thin reach: a table-keeper is a wonder if they ever arrive, and a test of Guest-reading courtesy. They will be fed. They will be asked about the Mother. They will not understand why a people need to be chosen in order to be wanted.
  • Threnmaieth (off the map). Not on the roll. A Yumboe in the Sacred Core is a diplomatic incident of manners, not a column. The crown cannot file a people who were never at a Tree. That is a relief and a slight at once.

The sentence that will not travel

On Kumbaan, the second sitting is how you belong. On the mainland, you belong because a town watched you Turn.

A table-keeper abroad who treats the second supper as kin-making is keeping Ndeyaan. The warden, the clerk, and the Book-hand who smile and file them as a guest are keeping their own rule for belonging. The sentence does not cross the storm-wall intact. Their claim stays. They say, “We were never lacking,” and they hold that a Tree on the isle would not have turned wanting into a gift the good people had missed. The injustice a table can meet now is smaller and sharper: hospitality that is nearly law on one side of the water is a proverb on the other, and romanticizing the Moon-Feast is itself a slight.

They do not keep the wardens’ scion-queue, the Book-hands’ year that fades when the poor cannot pay, or the Watchers’ recut beam. An attic-host is one person and one supper, with no authority outside the room.

Economy of the house

  • The hall is the living. On Kumbaan a table-keeper is fed by the same feast they host. The unseen hands make a poor house’s extra place possible. There is no stipend, no Cutting-leave, no pilgrimage-share.
  • An attic pays rent in the local coin. A Yumboe abroad works as anyone works. They may cook for a quay. They do not furnish a venting-hall.
  • No collection for a hall across the water. That would be a mission. There isn’t one.

A place-card

Badly copied by a Strandor sailor who did come back, and sold on a Lestrand quay as a dinner-grace. The verso is the sailor’s own hand: “for luck at second sitting.”

We have counted you. Sit. If you have a name we have not learned it yet; if you have a colour we have no tree to read it. The moon is enough light. Eat, and be the household. And if you sit again, you are not a stranger. This is not a proverb. Do not sell it as one.

On its face: hospitality, almost twee, already half-ruined by the quay-copy. The last two lines are the table-keeper’s, added when she saw him copy the blessing; he sold them anyway. The card is how Lestrand files the Njaalo: a Moon-Feast story, lucky if you eat twice. The woman in the attic still sets the place.

The Shore-Sitters

A second Kumbaan house. They belong to the isle, and they do not keep a mainland order. The Table-Keepers open the hall. These sit the storm-beaches. Almost no one reaches the isle. Someone still sits the beach for the wreck that does.

Everyday: shore-sitters, also wall-waiters. Own-name: Njawaal (njah-WAHL), a Yumboe coinage. Not Maiethren. Table-keepers call them the first night. The joke is that a wreck is not yet kin.

In one sentence: they sit the storm-shore with their back to the hill-hall and wait. They do not swim the wall, they do not test anyone with a colour, and if someone is still breathing they walk them uphill to a table.

If you meet one

A small person on a bad beach, lamp hooded so it does not call ships in. They look like they have been there a long time. They will not wade. They will take a living mainlander by the sleeve and start the path up. They will name a drowned body if the house leans Staying, or they will not, if it leans Serving, and they will still carry the body above the tide.

You would not call them a priest. You would find them by asking, “Who sits the black sand when the wall is loud?”

What they actually do

  • Sit. Storm nights, wreck-moons, the weeks after a crossing rumor. One person per stretch if the coast is kept at all. Reputation, not a roster.
  • Receive. Living: water, a blanket, no questions about Kind or colour. Dead: above the tide. Cargo: what the hall can use. They do not loot for a market. There is no quay.
  • Hand uphill. The living go to a table-keeper. First night as guest starts at the hall, not on the sand. The shore-sitter is why there was a first night.
  • Refuse the wall. They do not swim out. They do not keep a boat for rescue. The wall is not a door they walk. A mainlander who asks “why didn’t you come for me sooner?” is told, by the shore-sitter, “We do not call the sea. We wait.”

They do not keep the extra place (that is the hall). They do not Speak, stamp, or walk old mainland doors. They do not send anyone back across.

Who comes

Almost no one comes. A shore-sitter’s year may be empty. The job is still to sit. When someone does come, the whole valley hears it as news, not as a shipping schedule.

How they sit with the table-keepers

Same faith, two jobs. The shore decides alive. The hall decides kin. A shore-sitter who starts laying the extra place on the sand has started doing the other house’s work. A table-keeper who walks the black sand every storm has started doing this one. Both can happen in a thin year. Most years they are different people.

Said to a sailor who did come back

The night before he was walked uphill. Copied later as a quay-story, badly.

We did not fetch you. The wall does not owe you a hand. Sit until you can walk. If you walk, there is a hall. If you do not, we will still take you off the sand. Do not thank the sea. It was not kind. We were only here.

On its face: cold courtesy. The sailor heard a rescue. It was a wait.

The Slide

The lived face of illicit Tithe-supply. They are not clergy, and they are not one of the five faiths. They are the house for the week you cannot pay. They do not help you pay a Tithe. They keep you at the moment you start to fail, and they make that failure useful.

Everyday: the Slide, also week-keepers. Cant: the Vaethledd (VAYTH-led). Vaethorn says the last pot (or gift-credit, when the commons have run out). Lestrand says week-rent the way it says landlord. Threnmaieth files nothing; the roll already has a counted hall. A clerk who knows will say the late week.

In one sentence: they rent you not falling. One week of green, one outlet, one new page, and they are never in a hurry to let you get free.

They do not rewrite Bound Terms, steal scions, or sell a false Turning-witness.

Shape of the house

  • A practice with a key. There is no seat, no colour, no roster, no cellar liturgy. You are of the Slide when someone comes back next week. Reputation. When they die, the book of weeks goes to whoever still has a pot, a room, or a key.
  • One job. Furnish just enough Tithe-supply that the person does not become a public hazard, and never enough that they recover. The product is the week. The price is remaining theirs.
  • They call it rent. They extend. They keep a book. They will tell you, “I kept someone whole.” A kind face, a real key, a measured withhold later.
  • They are not the official halls. They do not stamp books, Speak colours, walk old doors, or open a hill-hall. They do not run the town greenhouse, the counted ael-hall, or a licence-guild. Those are the doors that make a person wait. The guilds will pretend not to know these people. That pretending is already part of the town.

If you meet one

They look like a landlord. A cousin who still has a pot after the commons ran dry. A junior clerk at a deathless house who will extend a novelty subscription one more week. A person with a spare room and a voice who will let you cry there, for a price that is never quite coin.

You would not call them a priest. You would find them by asking, “Who still has green when the leaf-lot is closed?”

They are useful the way a week is useful. They do not stamp a book, Speak a colour, or sell a false witness of a Turning.

Who comes, and when

Who Why they come What the week-keeper does What they will not do
A Taken-In cut off from land The greens-queue is longer than the wilt Rent a pot, a strip, a night on living dirt. Extend if the week cannot be paid. Will not give them a commons-share that lasts. Will not walk them to a warden to be filed as housed.
A Far-Voiced who lost a stage Isolated posting, dull labor, nowhere to vent. A feeling is about to break into everyone in earshot. That is the Cry. A room, an hour, a door that shuts. One outlet. The next week is the same week. Will not find them honest work. Will not let the Cry happen in the street. That brings the watch, and ruins the book.
A Long-Lived whose novelty has thinned The endowment is late; the fade has started A page, a letter, a gossip that is new enough. Rationed. Will not endow a library. Will not be their deathless patron in public.
An Unbound losing warmth Company costs; the hole is getting loud Sit with them. Be known, a little. Charge the sitting. Will not be family. Will not sign them a new Bargain. That is another table.
A Bound behind on civic coin Rent, a guild fee, a year that will not close Hold the debt. Extend. Use the hold. Will not rewrite Terms. Money, not a clause. Will not sell a cellar-easing.
A Returned whose Purpose pays nothing The work the dead want is not a trade A week of keep so they can keep walking. Will not furnish the Purpose. Will not send them to a plague-ward unless the ward pays them.
A family, before the town sees A child or a parent is sliding; they will pay so the square does not Take the week. Take the family’s coin. Keep the person quiet. Will not tell the warden. Will not make the slide a sermon.

An ordinary week

They extend first. That is the hook, and it is also what they believe about themselves. A person arrives already late. The week-keeper does not withhold on the first night. They open the lot, the room, the page. They write extended. They name a day.

The withhold comes later, and it is measured. Never enough to make a public hazard (a wilt in the square, a Cry in the market, a fade a neighbour can name), because that brings the watch, the guild, the counted hall, and the book dies. Never enough to let the person stand up and walk to a commons that would keep them. The asset is a person who is always almost whole.

They keep a book of weeks, not a ledger of coin. Coin is how some weeks are paid. The thing they are counting is how long you have not fallen, on their floor.

A child of the house learns the work by carrying water to the pots and by being told not to let a guest sleep a second week for nothing. There is no Seat to confirm it.

What they carry

A key. A pot, a spare room, a stack of pages that are new enough. A book of weeks. No stamp. No colour-scrap. No Cutting-leave. No false witness to sell in the square.

What they refuse

  • They will not pretend to be clergy. No colour, no book-stamp, no door-walk, no extra place.
  • They will not rewrite a Bound’s Terms. They may hold a Bound’s rent. That is money.
  • They will not steal a scion or sell I watched you Turn.
  • They will not push someone over the scary edge on purpose. A corpse, a public Cry, a wilt the town can see: a ruined week and a ruined name.
  • They will not run a mission, a cellar-church, or a Kumbaan export. There is almost no Tithe on the isle to own.
  • They will not call the week a gift. They will also not call it a cruelty. They call it a week.

Who may keep a week

Whoever has the pot and the book. A human, a Kitsune, a Selkie, or a Tengu who has a pot and a book can be of the Slide. A Yumboe abroad who charges for the extra place has broken the Open Table’s rule. That breach stays the table’s impiety. It does not make them a week-keeper.

By practice first. You are of the Slide when someone comes back next week, and you still have the key. There is no apprenticeship confirmed by anyone. A cousin who extended once and then let the person recover was kind. They are not of this house.

The week draws whoever already has the supply. Anyone with the key may still keep it. The house does not bar a Condition.

Act Often Why Also, and welcome
A pot, a strip, a night of dirt a Taken-In who kept a private lot they already furnish green a Kept with a window-box and no Tithe of their own
A shut room, an hour of voice a Far-Voiced with a spare hall they already know what an outlet costs a performer who lost the honest stage and kept the door
A page that is new enough a Long-Lived clerk, a junior at a deathless house novelty is already what their year runs on anyone who can still find a rumour the fade has not heard
The book of weeks itself whoever can read a late face the house is the count, not the crop a Kept broker, unencumbered, can move, can carry the book to the next town

The feared-licensed are not barred. A Stilled who rents stillness-hours is rare and tightly watched. A Bound who holds civic debt is money, not a Counterparty.

How they live with the other houses

House Everyday arrangement The hard edge
the Tree-Wardens Almost no contact. A warden furnishes a Tree, not a week. A Road-hand is not a week-cart. A scion is not Tithe-pay.
the Watchers A Watcher who sat an eve-vigil is not in this book. They do not sell a false watch.
the Book-Hands A Settler who marked owing has just made a customer. The clerk will watch them walk down the quay. The Slide does not stamp. The clerk does not furnish. The Bound they cannot help is still not this house’s Terms to rewrite.
the Door-Keepers A Struck named kin at a door still has a Tithe. Kinship is not green. A drowning-stone is not a rented lot.
the Table-Keepers No contact that matters. The second sitting is not a week. They do not charge for the extra place. That is a different faith’s one impiety.
Official halls and greens The official hall, the counted green, the licence. The town is glad someone “takes the late ones.” The same halls are called a public good, the crown’s channel, or the posted market, depending on the land. The Slide is the house they pretend not to know.

How they live in the three corners (and off the map)

  • Vaethorn (the last pot). This is where the pious version lives. The commons ran out. A cousin still has a pot. They will say they are tending a gift. The quiet obligation is the week: you are helped, and you are theirs. Public help here already shades toward service you cannot refuse. The last pot is that pressure, kept private.
  • Lestrand (week-rent). Ordinary. Posted, almost. A leaf-lot by the season is already the honest market. The Slide is the lot that will still open after the season-fee failed, at a price that is the next season. No sermon. A landlord.
  • Threnmaieth (the late week). The crown already holds Tithe. The Slide does not compete with the counted hall on ordinary days. They sell the week the hall is late, the extra green the roll does not cover, the novelty the archive will not release. They look like a clerk. The crown’s count is cold and precise. The Slide is the week that count cannot write down without admitting the channel failed. The roll pretends not to know.
  • Kumbaan. Almost no Given Tithes. A wreck walked uphill is a guest. The hall does not make them a debtor.

The week that will not end

Whoever supplies a Tithe that someone else depends on holds power over that person. The Slide is that power with a landlord’s face, and with a patience the official hall is not allowed.

The town is glad. A person who is quietly almost falling is not a Cry in the market. A devout neighbour can say the gift is still being tended. A crown clerk can say the channel held. The quay can say the owing was marked and someone took them in.

A kind Mother, if she is there, does not make the week fair. The need is the Tithe. People built the book of weeks.

They do not keep a scion-queue, a rewritten civic year, or a sentence that fails at the storm-wall. This house’s limit is the measured week: one key, one person, and no authority except the hold the Tithe gives them.

Adding a Term to a Bound, and selling a person who will swear they watched a Turning, are other trades. The Slide does not do either. Those trades are the Holding Desk and the Standing Trade.

Economy of the house

  • The week is the living. Paid in coin, in labor, in a favor, in a child sent to carry water. The book counts weeks first.
  • The official doors sit next to them. A town greenhouse, a counted ael-hall, an endowed long-house of books: those are the official sector. A week-keeper may sit next to all three and take the overflow.
  • Deathless proximity. A junior at a long-house who rations novelty is how a fortune that never dies feels on the street: not a loan of a century, a page at a time. The house does not run the bank.
  • No collection for a hall across the water. There isn’t one.

A week-slip

Lestrand leaf-lot, pinned inside a door that still opens after the season-fee has failed. The verso is the same slip, last week, the date scratched once.

One week of the lot. Extended. Do not let the pot dry. If you cannot come, send a child. The week is the same week. We do not call this a gift. We do not call it a debt. Come before the leaves turn. If you have already turned, come anyway.

On its face: a landlord being practical, almost kind. Underneath, the product is not falling. They think they refused the Mother’s word. They also refused the word “debt,” which is how the book stays a book of weeks and never quite closes. The line “If you have already turned, come anyway” is the house’s whole case: becoming did not save you from needing them. The child who carries water is learning the trade.

The Holding Desk

Everyday: the Holding Desk, also hold-clerks. Lestrand says it the way it says extension. Vaethorn has no clean word and calls it the second signing when a preacher wants a sin. Threnmaieth files nothing.

They are not the Slide. They do not rent green, an hour, or a page. They work in clauses.

A Bound finishes a civic year at an honest table. The year is payable. The Terms are not. The Term still hangs over them. That hanging Term is the Pledge, and the fear of it starts the next ask. The hold-clerk names a smaller Term that covers the larger one for a season. Each signing gives up more of the person. The accumulated Terms now hold the Bound. That last signing is the Given-Over. Guild speech calls it the Forfeit. The law has never decided when the person on the stool stopped being one.

Mutelo of the Lower Slip (moo-TEL-o) keeps the oldest chair on Orentel. They will tell you they kept a person walking. The book on the shelf is labelled “extensions.” The last page is the Forfeit.

They will not rent a Tithe-week. They will not sell the sentence “I watched you Turn.” They will not rewrite a Term the Counterparty has already named. They add a Term. They rest on that distinction: an added Term is not a rewritten one.

The Standing Trade

Citizenship is witnessed, not recorded. At a distance, the proof is a person who will speak. The unvouched will buy one.

Everyday: the Standing Trade. A buyer asks for “a standing.” A seller says “I was there,” and means they will say it later, in a square that was not theirs. Lestrand prices it. Vaethorn calls it false kin. Threnmaieth does not need it on ordinary days, because the roll is already the proof, and wants a bought witness badly when a name is missing.

They are not the Slide. They do not rent a week. They are not the Holding Desk. They do not write a Term. The product is a person who will stand and say they watched.

Nomele of the Hinge (no-MEL-eh) works the Old Crossing face — the Hinge Shore more often than Orentel, because a hinge has two sides and a standing has to travel. They do not sell paper. They sell who will walk with you to the next Tree.

The market fails in public when a voice still reaches a bought witness and they will not answer that Far-Voiced call, or when an honest Long-Lived is asked to Blood-Read the seller. They taste one drop of blood and see a true memory.

The Reckoned Offices

Everyday: the Reckoned Offices, or just the tablet offices. They are four jobs the Tallied Crown already had, now with names a traveller can ask for at the hall. Naming them did not found a church, and it did not found a country. They sit in the tablet-hall at Maiethlir. The grove and the First Seat are other buildings. Rithim is still town Speaker. These offices speak for the roll. They do not replace Rithim. The fight is the space between a colour Spoken in the square and a name written down.

Mouth Job What they want
Menirein (men-EE-rayn) registrar the roll complete. An unlisted child is a child the crown cannot keep hale.
Tarvae (TAR-vay) Reckoned Speaker Speaking and filing to stay one act. A colour Spoken and not written is a seeing that can be stolen.
Videm (VEE-dem) channel-clerk the counted hall, the tended green, and the hour of each, matched to the name on the roll.
Sirtal (SIR-tal) warrant-hand a channel that can be shut until the name is on the roll and stops changing.

They recruit by office and practice, not by Kind. Long-Lived memory is common at the tablet and is bent to the census rather than free to check it.

The Pourers

Everyday: the Pourers, also the Cup Circle. Neighbours say the five-year cup with the smile you use for a rich uncle’s hobby. The Motherfaith calls it private tending. The Fair Hand will not stamp it — there is no Counterparty they can see.

They are Long-Lived who keep a cup and say they poured years into it so the rest of a deathless life can still be walked. No colour falls. No warden Speaks it. The cup is not a Leaf-Fall, and they are not one of the five lived faiths.

Molevad (mo-LEV-ad) keeps the minutes of the best-known circle, on a long-house shelf in Lestrand library country. They look five years emptier and still themselves. They will tell you, “We invented the listener.” The face they say that listener wears changes from decade to decade. The clause does not. Years go in. Knowledge is kept. The cup does not Give a leaf and does not catch a death.

Reimaethe on the rise in Orentel poured four years into a cup and wants to stop before the fifth. Neighbours call it a hobby. That is the public fact.

The Walled Book and the Inner Close

Everyday: the Walled Book. The class that keeps it: the Closed Heirs. The place: Rothallo (roh-THAL-oh), Orenbren’s walled capital, one day’s walk from the Motherwood. People still say the Inner Close, and the Close. The walls answer to those names. It is the same city. It stays inside Orenbren, and it is not a realm of its own. Orenbren treats the walls as a guest-compact that never opened. Threnmaieth treats them as a roll that will not share ink. The First Seat stays in the wood. Rothallo is not the college.

This is political class rule. They do not Speak colours. They classify. Folk still say there was an older empire of the untithed, and that it sat on the grove. The part of that saying that holds is a gate that grew into a country. The everyday historical name is the Closed Seat. The walls are what is left.

Delamem (del-AH-mem) keeps the Book of Tithes. They will tell you, “The Book is housekeeping.” The page that assigns labour is in the same hand as the page that assigns a hearth-tax. Talnin of the Inner Close (TAL-nin) is a Closed Heir of the present generation. The name is on the heir-roll. Talnin’s tenth year, the Leaf-Fall year, is not finished.

A Closed child Given at the next Leaf-Fall is the house’s fear. Threnmaieth copying the Book onto the Threnhael is the other.

The Protectors (as the square knows them)

Everyday, when anyone speaks of them: the Protectors. The building the world is allowed to know: the Care. A hospice-endowment. Old money. A closed court. The story that travels is that the one Phoenix is being kept safe, and that is why no town has seen them in a generation.

Doctrine says there cannot be two. The Care still “holds the Phoenix.” A white-fire fall Spoken far away is filed as a mis-Speaking or a hush.

Vimein (vih-MAYN) keeps the outer book of the Care. They will say the Phoenix is in care. They will not produce them. They will offer a viewing of the closed court, a hymn that does not name a Mother, and a request that you not carry a story back to a square that has finally gone quiet.

You would take them for a hospice clerk. You would be right about the building.

What a neighbour can see: a hospice that endows its own heat and light; letters from wardens that do not come back answered; a generation of squares that speak of the Phoenix as already gone and as still in care, in the same breath.

How the Care stays warm is not told here.

Book IVHow People Live

Law and Citizenship

You belong to a town because that town watched you Turn. In your tenth year you stand at the town’s Turning Tree. The people see a coloured leaf fall on you, and a Condition is Given, or they see the Tree hug you, and you are one of the Kept: witnessed, and left without a Condition.

The Leaf-Fall is the most-attended day of a community’s year: kin, neighbours, the tree-wardens, all gathered. That is the tenth-year day.

So citizenship in the Turning is witnessed, not recorded. You are of a place because that place remembers you. It watched you become who you are.

There is no register of the people. No town, church, or crown keeps a list of everyone and their gift. A list like that would be a record held on people. Belonging is the memory of the people who stood there. The one kingdom that does keep such a list is a notorious exception: Threnmaieth, the Tallied Crown. People find it unsettling, because no one else does it.

This is how belonging works in the Years of Hands. That is the present age, and a town Tree is the ordinary place to Turn. In the Walking Years, people still had to walk to Thaeloren. That is the Awakening Tree. The crowd that saw you Turn was the crowd under it, or no one, if you never arrived. Someone who saw you there, or a word from that someone, can still carry the witness home.

In a village the witness is the whole settlement. In a city it is a hearth — a street, a quay-gang, a courtyard, a guild-stair — not twenty thousand mouths pretending to remember one child. The legal fact does not change.

Turning in a city: the hearth stood

A village of three hundred can remember every child. A salt-city of tens of thousands cannot. At that scale the witness is nested: a smaller circle inside the city, still the people who saw the child, and still no register.

The Tree is still at the centre. Turning-Week is still the week the town keeps for the rite. The square is still full. The legal fact is that your hearth stood. Naming the city only says where that hearth sits.

A hearth is eighty to two hundred mouths who already know the child. They bring their ten-year-olds in a named slot of the week. The warden speaks the colour, saying aloud the leaf’s colour and so the Condition, or names the hug. The city as a crowd saw a crowd. The hearth saw a child.

One pillar of that hearth — a Long-Lived, a Bound, or a Far-Voiced who stood — carries the memory when a far desk asks. Kin and neighbours carry it first; the pillar is how that memory travels. An orphan is still brought by kin, by the household that feeds them, or by the warden-hearth. A child no hearth will bring has missed the Turning, the same as in a village.

To prove you are of a city, you name a hearth and produce someone of it, or a pillar who stood. The person says, “I am of the third quay.” That sentence is citizenship. “I am of Orentel” names a place on the map.

Threnmaieth writes down who stood. That is still the aberration. Everyone else keeps the week’s slate as hours: which hearth stands at which bell. The slate does not record the gift.

How you prove who you are: by people, not papers

Trust runs through people who cannot be faked, so you prove yourself by a person, not by a paper from a state.

You are known by witnesses. Everyday speech calls this standing-for. In Vaethorn, the Waiting Lands, someone vouches for you at worship, and they call that the speaking. In Lestrand, the Ledger Coast, they call a commercial guarantee warranting. In Threnmaieth, the Tallied Crown, you are named to the crown, and they call that the naming.

  • A Long-Lived remembers you, and their memory is incorruptible and runs back centuries.
  • A Bound vouches for you. A Bound’s sworn word is literally binding, so a false vouch is not a lie they can cheaply tell.
  • A Far-Voiced speaks for you. Their voice betrays real feeling under strain, so they cannot smoothly lie on your behalf.

To establish yourself in a new place is to be vouched into it. You acquire people who will stand for you. The friendless and the rootless are the truly vulnerable, because they have no one to vouch. The law shuts out the unvouched.

The customs below keep a traveler vouched.

How a traveler stays vouched

The unvouched remain the vulnerable class. A legitimate traveler moves on someone’s word. The road still works the way it did when the witness had to come home from Thaeloren. The person who saw you, or their word, still has to be able to answer.

A watch that stops a stranger asks three things: name, last hearth, who stands. Answer any two and the road continues. Answer none and you are held until dawn, or until someone will stand, or you are walked to the next gate. The watch presses the person who can give none of the three. A company that can name a last host and show one mark is let through. An adventuring party uses the same three answers.

Custom What it is Where it stops
Road-word A named person at origin or last town. You tell the watch, “Ask Halim of the third quay.” A Far-Voiced can call; a Bound can send a word; a letter is a token of a mouth. The word holds while that person lives and will stand.
Company-vouch A party stands for each other. One locally known or ticketed member covers the rest for passage. You may sleep. You are not of this town.
Guild-mark A stiller’s ticket, a Crae journeyman-mark from the element-guilds, a Voice-ticket. Craft marks travel. The mark says you may Gaze, work an element, or Cry in a hall. Leave for that use, and nothing further.
House-note A note from a deathless house. The Long-Lived who founded it still runs the bank. Credit that also stands for you, lightly. Most travelers carry none.
Guest-right A host who takes you in stands for the stay. An inn, a table, a road-house. You say, “I ate at X,” and it holds about a week. The host must have taken you in.
Walk-custom Someone on a named walk (Near Mile, Salt Walk, Long Mile, First-Hand year) is vouched as a walker by the road: mile-shrines, road-houses, a berth-slate. When the walk ends, the word ends.

A person Struck on the road is vouched anew at the next desk that will take them. If the change is dangerous, that desk is the Intake. The guild takes them in because the hazard is already live. Changing without witnesses still leaves a gap someone must speak for. Having changed is lawful.

People still sell a vouch under no name. That sale sits outside the customs in the table. If the bought speaker will not answer when a Far-Voiced calls, the sale fails where everyone can hear it.

Kumbaan, the Sundering Isle, keeps no mainland road-word. You arrive unknown. A road-word, a company’s vouch, or a guild-mark still helps you leave a quay on Strandoren. That is the continent across the water. None of those make you known on the hill. On the isle, the right to be a guest is a seat at the table. A second sitting makes you kin of that table. A mainland watch will not take that table as the last hearth you name when they ask where you slept.

The Struck: those who changed unwitnessed

A Struck person acquired their Condition later, and alone: a drowning, a death, a sworn contract, long after their tenth-year Turning. The town watched everyone else become who they are. No one watched the Struck. They changed with no witnesses.

So a Struck person must be vouched anew. The community has to re-know them as what they have become, on the word of those who will stand for them. The old unease that the Struck are “a little suspect” is not only prejudice. Their becoming happened where no one could see it. A change everyone witnessed is trusted; a change that happened in the dark must be spoken for. Every polity has people whose transformation went unwitnessed, and every polity feels differently about that.

Two facts make that gap harder to close:

  • The dangerous Struck arrive raw. A Struck Stilled or Far-Voiced manifests untrained, with the hazard already live and no guild to have caught it young. For these, being vouched-in is bound up with mandatory guild-intake. You are taken in hand and apprenticed at once, for everyone’s safety, whether you wanted the vocation or not.
  • Some Struck changes sit beside a crime. The Bound are Struck by a sworn contract; the Returned by dying. To vouch a Bound into a community is to acknowledge a contract no one witnessed being sworn, which is why contract-law and the acceptance of the Bound are tangled together.

Vaethorn’s second word for the Struck is the late-come: met at a later door. Lestrand’s is the turned: a fact, said plainly. Threnmaieth’s is the unwitnessed: suspect until named.

The first hour is care. The person has changed, and nobody has a leaf-colour for them yet. The formal words can wait.

On a quay, the crew hauls the person up, counts the breaths, and does not walk them to the Tree that night. If a clerk opens a book over someone who cannot yet refuse a line, whoever is holding the person’s head shuts the book. In a devout house, the family takes the person inside and does not take them to the Tree’s roots tonight. A sibling who needs to may say “Maieth” over the blanket. Maieth is the short name devout people use for the Leaf-Mother. The person under the blanket is not required to say it. In a waiting town, they get the person dry and, if the person can sit, to the common pot. Nobody names a leaf-colour over the steam. The wet leaf on the town spring is not a sign for this hour. On the Sundering Isle, which is rare, Njunda feeds them. Nobody gives them a test, and nobody tells them the mainland story about the Mother.

If the change came by an oath, take the pen out of the hand until the person can hear the clause they are still agreeing to. If the change came by a death that did not finish, keep them warm, and do not assign the unfinished work that night. Being acknowledged at a Tree, settling their civic standing, or walking them back to an Old Ways door are later days. That door is the older faith’s own household rite. If they are still inside their tenth year and Turning-Week is still open, bring them to be welcomed. Do not stand them up as if the Tree were giving them a second life.

The Inviolate Will

Every polity holds one principle as the floor of law, and it holds because it is true: no Condition can compel, control, or bend another person’s will. That is the Inviolate Will. The Bound’s Whisper, the Far-Voiced’s Cry, and the Long-Lived’s regard cannot cross it: every Condition’s danger stops at property, pain, and fear, at the edge of another’s choosing. Every child is taught this maxim:

“The will is one’s own.”

Vaethorn keeps the maxim as the Mother’s own line, and reads it as scripture. Lestrand cites the free-will clause, and treats it as ordinary commercial law. Threnmaieth cites the crown’s assurance, and uses that citation to justify the roll.

A Tithe is the upkeep a Condition requires from the person who carries it.

  • The law has no charge for one Condition seizing another’s mind, because a Condition cannot do it. The Conditioned are charged as neighbours on that ground, and everyone knows the limit.
  • Harm done with a Condition splits in two. Negligent Tithe-lapse (letting your element vent, your Cry break, your green go to seed) is a regulatory or public-nuisance matter. Willful weaponizing (deliberately turning a gift on someone) is ordinary violent crime, no gentler for the instrument. In practice only the Stilled’s gaze supports a charge the hearing can speak as “you petrified a citizen.” Most of what the law deals with here is negligence. Assault is the smaller share.
  • A Bound can say, truthfully, “my contract cannot make you do anything.” Whether a given polity listens is where they diverge.

The safety-licence: how hazard is regulated (and by whom)

The law attaches to a particular person in one place: public safety. A ticket attaches to a hazardous use, not to being Conditioned. Across the world, a licence attaches where a practice is dangerous to others. Power alone does not require a licence. The paper names the use.

Everyday speech calls this leave the ticket. In Vaethorn a practitioner asks for the blessing-to-practice. In Lestrand they show the guild-ticket. In Threnmaieth they show the crown-warrant.

The three ticket-Conditions are the Stilled, Far-Voiced, and the Answered (~7 / ~12 / ~10). Together that is ~29% of people, ~39% of the Conditioned. They can be asked for a ticket if they practice the hazardous use. Those shares are people who can be asked. They are not names on a crown list.

A ticket names a competence (stiller, hall-voice, Crae-hand), the work you may do. It does not name the Condition as an identity. Three guilds keep no shared roll. The state watches the charter.

Who Ticket names Who is not on it
the Stilled — personal Gaze-ticket Anyone who Gazes in public: surgeons, riot-lines, demolition. Most stillers hold one, because the vocation is the Gaze. A Stilled who never Gazes (rare). The guild still wants them in the yard.
Far-Voiced — Voice-ticket / hall-hour Those who Cry in a crowd or keep a scheduled hour: criers, performers, sworn witnesses. Hall-keepers stamp that you are competent and sell you the hour at the same door. A field-voice who lets the Cry out at the hedge. The hall-slate lists hours. A Far-Voiced who never takes an hour is not on it.
the Answered — Crae-ticket / yard-leave Element used in a shop, a yard, a hull. The Crae stamp the craft. Those are the element-guilds. An Answered who never works the element in public.
Bound — a table, not a body-ticket No licence. The honest table oversees Terms. The Whisper cannot command. The Bound’s share, about 5% of people, sits at that contract table. It is separate from the licence rate.
No ticket — Two-Bodied, Long-Lived, Returned, the Taken-In, the Unbound, Phoenix, and all the Kept. About two people in three. They are simply neighbours.

Active papers in a working city are smaller than that 29% — perhaps half of it, fewer on the frontier, where a feared Condition is as likely to be hanged as to be licensed at the ticket-stool. That guild seat stamps the paper.

Licences are guild-issued, not state-issued. A guild knows its own members the way any craft guild does. The roll is a roster of practitioners of a dangerous art, kept by the art’s own masters. The state charters the guild and lets it police its own under threat of losing the charter. No single authority holds the Conditioned. Guilds guard monopolies, tickets become property, and an unlicensed practitioner threatens the guild’s charter as much as the public’s safety.

Harm, the watch, and the hearing

The Inviolate Will already sorts the charge: negligent Tithe-lapse versus willful weaponizing. This is the ladder the charge walks. There is no crime of being a Condition.

  1. Neighbour-word. Most harm dies on the street. Restitution, apology, a Bound word if they have one. The watch is not called.
  2. The watch. Night-watch, ward-watch. Often the Kept, whose nights are not spent on a Tithe, and the Two-Bodied, whose ranging is the Tithe, with a Far-Voiced for the shout. They hold a body until morning. They do not investigate Conditions. They fetch a pillar or a guild.
  3. Guild-hearing. Tithe-lapse, unlicensed Gaze, an element let go in a shop. The guild fines, suspends a ticket, binds to apprenticeship. The state is not in the room unless the charter is at risk.
  4. Open hearing. Willful harm. Witnesses — especially the three pillars. The charge is the act. The hearing can say “You Gazed a man,” and that is assault. “You are Stilled” is not a charge. A Gaze used as a forever-hold is the rare charge the Stillers’ own code already names.
  5. What the hearing can do without writing you down:
    - Restitution — coin, labor, furnish a Tithe you broke
    - Ticket-stripping — you may not practice the dangerous act
    - A Bound word, or a term
    - Exile from the hearth — you become unvouched in your own city. This is the civic death. It does not require a file.
    - The rope or the wall — murder, and the rare forever-hold. Used more often on the frontier. Rare in the Warm.

A Watchful crown writes you down. That crown keeps a census. That extra record is their extra punishment, and it is why a Watchful town feels like a different country.

The watch holds you until morning. You sit. In a village the watch is whoever is awake. In Maiethlir, the Tallied Crown’s seat, the watch is the Down Gate. In Orentel, the salt-quay, the watch is the night-gate on the quay. In Eolvaeth, the waiting-lands seat, the square watches, and in a year when the town sends its children on, the camp-streets watch instead. Threnmaieth’s roll belongs to the clerks. The watch in a Watchful town can be told to hold someone the roll wants. The holding is still until morning unless a clerk comes.

A town sends a cohort when its own Hand cannot Turn the children this week. The Hand is that town’s Turning Tree. The group is kin, the children who are ten, and a warden’s word. Eolvaeth has sent one toward Saelvaeth in years when its own wood did not Turn the children that week. The Mill-hold sends its ten-year-olds toward Brenthael when its Tree is unsound. Between sends, the people are at their stalls. A sent cohort from Eolvaeth stops at the thorn outside the vale and eats before they go on, even when the path is clear. They will not say why.

The Closing left that wall and a book. The Closing was the war that ended behind Rothallo’s wall. The wall is Rothallo, still called the Inner Close. Rothallo is Orenbren’s capital. No one kept the riders as a host that could be called again. The Hinge Hush left a rate, a west-water clause, and no named winner. The Hinge Hush is the peace on the Old Crossing. It left no fleet. Dock-bells on the Hinge Shore still pause in Hale-month, then go back to work. Netstrand still keeps hulls for hire. A charter is a crossing with a number on it. The fifteen powers do not keep standing armies.

A door-custom, pinned inside a Lestrand night-gate. No crest. The verso is last week’s rain.

Name. Last hearth. Who stands. Two will do. None, and you sit the bench until the second bell, or until a mouth comes, or we walk you to the next gate. We do not ask your colour. We do not keep the three answers past dawn. If you are on a walk, say which. If you ate at the bed-house, say so. If you have a ticket, show the stool-mark, not a story. The bench is not a cell. The bench is until morning.

The stool-mark is the stamp from the ticket-stool. The notice is a watch doing the practical work. The three questions are how this gate checks belonging. The writer believes that not asking your colour is what sets this gate apart from Threnmaieth. The gate also does not write the answers down, and that is how a city of tens of thousands still runs on hearths.

The three pillars: why the Conditioned are the legal system

Three Conditions are the legal apparatus itself, and they are the people who make a register unnecessary:

  • Long-Lived: incorruptible memory. The archivists, notaries, and judges. A matter of record is whatever they say they witnessed a century ago. Where documents would be forged, a Long-Lived simply remembers.
  • Bound: binding word. A Bound’s sworn oath is literally binding and they hear a lie as it is told, so they are the witnesses, oath-keepers, and debt-arbiters. When a treaty must truly hold, a Bound swears it.
  • Far-Voiced: unfakeable feeling. Trusted as sworn witnesses because their voice betrays real emotion under strain, so they cannot lie well on the stand.

Evidence and contract in the Turning are therefore made of people. Memory that does not corrupt, an oath that hears a lie, and testimony that cannot fake a feeling are enough. The Turning does not write everyone down.

Rights of the Kept

The Kept are the quiet centre of citizenship, and their legal position is unremarkable. They were witnessed at their Turning like everyone. They carry no Tithe, need no licence, answer to no guild. In most polities the Kept are the default legal person. The rest of the law is drawn around them.

Vaethorn’s second word is the Waited, or Mother-kept: people speak it with pity, as if the Tree passed them over. Lestrand’s is the free: labor with no Tithe attached, and Lestrand says that with approval. Threnmaieth’s is the unmarked, or the passed-over, and the clerks note it.

Two facts sit under that quiet position:

  • Kept status is not permanent immunity. About a third of the Kept are still Struck later. So a Kept person lives untithed and unlicensed but never wholly outside the safety system’s reach. The day they are Struck, they too must be vouched anew. Legally, “Kept” describes your present, not a promise about your future.
  • Where reverence for the gift runs hot, the Kept can be quietly pitied or pressured: treated as having “missed out,” leaned on to explain why the Tree passed them over. This is a social harm the law rarely names, and it runs hardest in the most devout polities.

The Given keep their plain name everywhere. Vaethorn’s second word is the gifted, said as a blessing. Lestrand’s is the Given, said as a plain fact. Threnmaieth’s is the marked, because the name goes on the roll.

Edge cases the law must hold

  • The Hollow (the Unbound) have lost the Pledge they paid and been freed from the contract, and an isolated Hollow is pulled to sign again. The law has to decide whether they still have the capacity to be held to a new contract, and whether someone must protect them from one. Kinder polities extend a protective status (a guardian, a claim on connection, a bar on presenting them fresh contracts). Harsher ones leave them to the pull.
  • The Given-Over (the “Forfeit”) is the setting’s sharpest legal wound: a Bound who kept re-signing until the counterparty effectively owns the person. This is a living case of legal personhood erased by accumulated contract, and the contract-guilds have a term of art for it because the law has never fully decided at what point a person has bargained away enough of themselves to stop being one. The question comes back, and it stays unresolved.
  • Phoenix is a political matter. One legendary person who cannot hide is a symbol no ruler controls. Whoever currently wants the one alive tries to own them or protect them.
  • A child who missed, refused, or never stood is UnTurned. They have no hug and no colour. Folk will call them Kept. The law files them as the person the town raised, vouched if someone will stand, unwitnessed at the Tree. A second Leaf-Fall is not a remedy. Struck doors still open later.

Three polity stances

The rules above hold in every polity. The tone changes from place to place. Variation comes from three choices every polity makes separately:

  • Theology: how gift-devout is it? From ardent Motherfaith gift-integration (a Condition is a blessing, belonging is a birthright) through worn-lightly civic religion to frank secular pragmatism, and the odd local heresy. This decides how the Struck, the Kept, and the feared Conditions are read before the law even speaks.
  • Reach: Tree-rich core or Tree-poor edge? Where healthy Trees stand, reach is full and the Given are dense. Where Trees are few, young, or sickening, the population runs higher on the Kept and the Struck.
  • Governance: how far does the state reach onto the person? From light guild self-rule, the usual arrangement (the craft polices its own, the crown charters the guild), all the way to a crown that writes its subjects down. This decides how far a safety licence becomes a way of watching people.

Three recurring points on these choices:

The Warm (high theology). Law flows straight from the Motherfaith’s gift-theology: a Condition is a blessing, the Struck are vouched-in graciously as people the Mother met at a later door, licences are framed as helping you tend your gift. Warmest near the Awakening Tree. Its injustice is soft and hard to name: everyone is expected to serve their gift, so guild “vocation” shades into pressure, the Kept get pitied for missing out, and to decline your Condition’s obvious use reads as ingratitude to the Mother herself.

The Watchful (governance at its extreme: a deliberate aberration). At the far end of the governance choice sits a crown that breaks the world’s norm and does write its people down: a ruler who does not trust living memory and keeps a roster of every subject and their Condition. Every polity has guild rolls, notarial acts, and tax ledgers. The aberration is the further record: a census of persons and their gifts, a record held on people. Threnmaieth’s census calls itself the Threnhael (THREN-hayl): thren (sacred counting) + hael (whole, hale), “the keeping-whole.” Clerks say they “keep the realm hale.” Subjects are “kept whole.” Outsiders hear “the Counting.” At home the crown calls it prudence and order. The people who live under it live under surveillance. The unvouched are hunted, the Struck are pursued to be catalogued, Tithe-needs become points of leverage, and the Bound especially are watched far past their real hazard. The Whisper cannot command, and the roster’s clerks know it. The extra watch is stigma carried out as safety. A Watchful kingdom that is also devout and Tree-rich is the darkest mix of the three choices.

The Frontier (low reach). Far from any healthy Tree, witnessing is thin and custom outruns law. The giving thins where the Trees are thin, so these lands run higher on the Kept and higher on the Struck: more people the rite reached faintly or not at all. No one asks for a licence. The Taken-In, whom cities keep at arm’s length, are neighbours here, and they are the backbone of the frontier economy. An unvouched Struck person may have no one to stand for them and no guild to train them, and a feared Condition may meet a rope where a city would have issued a licence. Reach and governance often move together. A thin-reach edge tends to be thinly governed too. A devout frontier and a lightly-governed core both exist.

Economy and the Tithe

Every Condition has a Tithe. That is an ongoing need that must be met, or the danger comes out. About three people in four carry a Condition, so keeping them able to carry it is part of how the economy is built. What began as a private need becomes a public one at that scale.

Tithe-provision is a permanent civic utility, like wells and roads: visible in every town, fought over, load-bearing for the people who need it. Grain is the other supply. Grain feeds everyone, every day. The greens and halls keep an exposed minority from sliding into the Condition’s danger, and keep their neighbours from living next to that slide. The sector is large enough to organize a town around. It is not the food supply.

Most of the Conditioned pay the Tithe by working: the need and the job are the same act. A smaller share must be furnished. The people who furnish it and the people who need it are often the same. Whoever controls the furnishing holds power.

Who actually buys provision

The figures below are shares of work and of custom.

Who About What “provision” is
Self-paying by vocation the Answered ~10 · Two-Bodied ~18 · the Stilled ~7 → ~35% Work is the Tithe. They are a labor market.
If the work fits Far-Voiced ~12 · Returned ~7 → ~19% Self-pay if the job fits. Buy a hall-hour or carry the need unpaid when it does not. The hour is booked for a Far-Voiced voice.
Provided-for the Taken-In ~13 · Long-Lived ~2.5 · the Unbound ~1.5 · Bound Terms ~3.5 → ~20% Green, novelty, warmth, whatever the clause set.
The Kept ~25% No Tithe. They buy bread. They do not buy a lot-hour. That is an hour on the city’s green.

Green is free on a farm. Most Taken-In live where it is free. The purchased or civic customer base — urban Taken-In, hall-users without a stage, novelty-seekers, warmth that money can only approximate — is on the order of a seventh of the people, concentrated in stone towns. The queues are real. A city fights over lot-hours and hall-bells the way it fights over wells: a wilt or a Cry in the square is a public problem. Tithe-goods do not move in the grain carts.

Self-paying vs provided-for Tithes

Tithes do not all cost the same to pay. Some cost nothing in coin, because paying them is doing useful work. That sorts the Conditioned from self-paying to provided-for, and almost everything else follows from where a Condition sits. Which side a person lands on can depend on their vocation, not only their Condition.

Self-paying Tithes: the work is the payment. For several Conditions, the upkeep is discharged by doing exactly the labor society needs. Their maintenance and their productive output are the same act.

Condition The Tithe …paid by doing
the Answered use the element in earnest the craft itself: a Tide-Answered shipwright slakes the Tithe by building the ship
Two-Bodied spend time as the animal the ranging work: herding, rescue, courier, scout; being the beast is the job
the Stilled burn off the Grey by exertion. The Grey is the residue each Gaze leaves active labor: surgery, riot-lines, demolition; the vigorous work burns that Grey off

If the work fits, the Tithe is self-paying. If it does not, someone has to furnish it. Two Conditions sit in both columns. The job decides which.

Condition The Tithe Self-pays when… Falls to provided-for when…
Far-Voiced vent strong emotion each day the vocation is the vent: performer, crier, negotiator, sworn witness; feeling in earnest on the job discharges the Tithe the voice has no outlet (isolated posting, dull labor, silence) and they need a venting-hall. That hall is furnished so the voice can be let out
Returned act toward the Purpose. That is the unfinished task they still have to finish the Purpose aligns with the work: plague wards, recovery, tending the dead; doing the deadly job pays it the Purpose is personal or unmet by any trade (a vengeance, a vow, a person to find). No employer can furnish it, and it pulls against ordinary work

Provided-for Tithes: upkeep that must be supplied. For the rest, the Tithe is a cost with no productive by-product. Someone must furnish the green, the novelty, the warmth, and that furnishing is the Tithe-provision sector.

Condition The Tithe …must be provided
the Taken-In rest on living land green: trivial on the farm, a real expense in a stone city (potted groves, leave to use a garden)
Long-Lived learn something genuinely new novelty: books, travel, correspondence, the traffic of a university town
the Unbound keep connecting to others warmth: company, being known; the one Tithe money buys least well
Bound keep the Terms whatever the counterparty set: idiosyncratic, sometimes ruinous

The self-payers make a living because their Condition is a trade. A living is not the same thing as an easy life. The Stilled self-pays and is still the most tightly licensed person in the world. The Answered self-pays under heavy guild discipline. Self-paying buys a living. It does not buy social comfort. The provided-for are economically exposed: they depend on someone furnishing their upkeep, and whoever furnishes it holds power over them. The people whose Tithe depends on the job are the most precarious of all. A Far-Voiced who loses their stage, or a Returned whose Purpose turns personal, can slide from self-paying to exposed overnight.

Tithe-poverty and the safety net

Because provided-for Tithes cost money, you can be too poor to stay whole. A Taken-In who can’t afford green, a Long-Lived with no access to anything new, a Far-Voiced with nowhere to vent: these people suffer want, and they slide toward the danger: the wilting, the fade, the involuntary Cry. Destitution here has a visible, dangerous face.

Who pays for Tithe-provision is the political question, and polities answer it differently:

  • Public good. Warm polities treat Tithe-provision like roads or wells (town greenhouses, free venting-halls, endowed libraries) because a town whose Conditioned stay whole is a town that stays safe and productive. The pious framing is the same practice: help everyone tend their gift.
  • Private burden. Harsher polities leave it to the individual and the guild, and the Tithe-poor fall, becoming a genuine public hazard, which the same polities then police rather than prevent.
  • Leverage. Provide it, and hold it. An employer, guild, or crown that furnishes your Tithe can withhold it.

Labor: who does what

The Tithe and the vocation are usually the same shape. The Condition is the job:

  • Two-Bodied (~18%, the plurality). The versatile backbone of physical labor: herding, haulage, rescue, courier, scouting, war. Runs in bloodlines (whole “wolf-folk” / “hawk-folk” families), so the family trade and the family Condition are one inheritance.
  • the Taken-In (~13%). The food-and-timber base: farmers, foresters, herders, wardens. The frontier economy runs on them, because they thrive on living land. Cities need their grain and keep a working minority of them. Arm’s length is how the city treats them. It is not an empty workplace.
  • Far-Voiced (~12%). Coordination and trust at a distance: the relay of criers posted on coasts, roads, and borders, plus performers, negotiators, and sworn court witnesses. Criers posted along those roads give the country a live signal from coast to border.
  • the Answered (~10%). The elemental crafts, guilded by element: Ember (smiths, glasswrights), Tide (shipwrights, dyers), Root (masons, potters), Gale (sailmakers, millers); plus firefighting and rescue rotations.
  • Returned (~7%). The work that kills the living, done by those death can’t take twice: plague wards, mine and cave-in recovery, deep and dangerous salvage, and tending the dead.
  • the Stilled (~7%). Precision stillness: surgeons and medics, peacekeepers on riot-lines, controlled demolition and stonework. Licensed and guild-disciplined.
  • Long-Lived (~2.5%). The memory professions: archivists, notaries, judges, scholars, and bankers. They are few. If many more people never died, those professions and the banks would lock up around them.
  • Bound (~5%). The enforcement and contract class: oath-keepers, debt-arbiters, treaty-swearers, witnesses whose word is literally binding. The Unbound sit inside this same contract work. A released Bound goes back to whatever they did before, and needs connection to keep at it. They are not a separate workforce.
  • Phoenix (a trace). No characteristic trade. A political symbol.

And the Kept (~25%). The Kept carry no Tithe, so they have no upkeep cost, and no Condition, so they have no built-in trade. Economically that makes them the free generalist labor: unburdened by any Tithe-need, unclaimed by any Condition-guild, able to take any ordinary work and move where they like. Where the faithful pity the Kept for missing a gift, they are still the least encumbered people in the world.

Why Taken-In live in stone cities

Green is an expense in a stone city, and cities keep the Taken-In at arm’s length because they thrive on the frontier. Both are true. Arm’s length is how polite districts stand toward the bark and antler a Taken-In carries on the body. The lots are still staffed.

They are in the city because:

  1. They were Given here. A copper-green leaf, the Taken-In’s colour, at a city Tree. Family, work, and a hearth-witness keep them. Leaving the city is leaving the circle that saw you Turn.
  2. Grain and timber come in; people come with them. Seasonal Taken-In on the quays, wintering, some staying. The harvest-hands who do not go home in Hale-month. Hale-month is the spring month, also called Haelren.
  3. The lot is a job. Greens-keepers need Taken-In labor. The city imports green-skill for the commons, then will not sit them at the high table.
  4. The door can open in a city’s old green. Lost and dying in a deep wild is the Struck path that makes a Taken-In. A walled grove, a lot at night, a river-garden after a flood can open the same door. It is rare, and it is enough.
  5. They cannot afford to leave. Green-poverty is a trap: you need the lot to stay whole, the lot is gated, you work for lot-hours, you never save the cart-fare home. That trap is the city’s green trade inside the Slide. That is the illicit market in Tithe-goods. Week-keepers sell it, and the green is what they sell when the gate will not.

World share is still ~13%. A stone city runs lower — a twentieth to a twelfth of its adults, concentrated on lots, orchards, river-gardens, and the week-keeper’s pot. That private pot of green is what the same trade rents by the week. Enough for a queue. Enough for a wilt two streets off. Enough for a customer at that pot. The frontier still has the density and the honour. The city has the gate.

Banking and the deathless houses

The Turning’s banks do not die. These are the deathless houses. A Long-Lived founded the bank and is still personally running it two centuries later, the same memory, word, and ledger, unbroken. Merchants carry letters of credit. That is a written promise to pay in place of a chest of coin. Three things follow.

  • The trusted note. A note issued by a deathless house is trusted almost as coin, because everyone knows the banker cannot forget a debt, cannot be quietly succeeded by a defaulting heir, and will personally answer for it in fifty years. Their paper circulates further and holds value better than any crown’s. Other paper is trusted by how close it comes to the word of a house that will not die.
  • The long instrument. Deathless houses lend and endow on horizons no mortal lender would risk: century loans, multi-generation trusts, the patient financing of cathedrals, canals, and universities. The instruments are letters of credit and endowments. The world has no stock exchange and no joint-stock company.
  • The concentration risk. Wealth that never passes to an heir never disperses through inheritance. The scattering that a death forces on every other fortune never happens here. Even at ~2.5% of the population, the deathless can slowly gather an outsized share of standing capital. Their small numbers are what keep that share from becoming the whole of standing capital, and the economy never fully resolves the gathering. An endowment sends the coin out and keeps the steering of it, so influence concentrates even while the cash flows into the institutions they still direct.

Many houses began as road-houses in the Walking Years: inns, beds, and century-credit for that road. In that age, families still had to walk to Thaeloren. That is the Awakening Tree. When the core no longer required the walk, the same Long-Lived still remembered every debtor, and the paper outlived the pilgrimage season. The houses became banks. Two of them are still standing, on opposite errands. The White Note House collects on the old paper. The Third Hearth still makes the bed. The trusted note is an instrument from the Walking Years that outlived its road.

Inheritance and the problem of not dying

Inheritance law is bent by the deathless. A Long-Lived’s children do not inherit the Condition. They age and die while the parent does not. So a deathless person outlives every heir, and their estate never enters the inheritance stream at all. A fortune that is never passed down still has to go somewhere.

The characteristic answer: the deathless endow. Having no heirs, and needing for their own Tithe a steady supply of novelty (new books, new scholarship, new things to learn), a Long-Lived characteristically funds the institutions that generate knowledge: libraries, universities, archives. The deathless banker becomes the deathless patron, and pays their own Tithe by building the places that feed it. It is also how they grieve: an endowment in the name of a child who died a century ago. An endowment is coin converted into permanent, directed patronage. The founder does not die, so they keep the chair, name the scholars, and steer the institution for centuries. The fortune has not been let go. Endowment eases the coin concentration, because liquid wealth flows out into public works, and deepens the influence concentration, because the same few deathless hands still guide the universities, courts, and archives their money built. Keeping the Long-Lived few does not end that lock-up. The lock-up changes shape, from hoarded coin to patronage that does not step down.

For everyone else inheritance is ordinary, except the Two-Bodied bloodlines, where the Condition, the family animal, and often the family trade descend together, so a guild place and a Condition can be a single inheritance. And the law’s sharpest open question is economic at root: the Given-Over. A Bound contracted away so much of themselves that the counterparty effectively owns the person. That is the ultimate inheritance failure: an estate, and a self, absorbed by a creditor.

Center and periphery

The economy is not evenly spread. Near the mature Trees, in the core, Conditions are dense, guilds are strong, and specialized Condition-work and its goods are the great export. Out at the margins, on the frontier, the giving thins: more of the population are generalist Kept or untrained Struck, fewer are Given specialists. So:

  • The core exports specialized work and finished goods (Answered crafts, Stilled medicine, deathless-house credit) and imports raw staples.
  • The frontier leans hardest on the Taken-In, who thrive there and are neighbours, and on generalist Kept labor. It exports food, timber, and raw materials, and imports the specialists it can’t grow. Trade friction is real. Distance, spoilage, and the thin reach all mean the frontier is specialist-poor and self-reliant. Cities still keep a working Taken-In minority behind a gate.

The shadow economy

A gate on Tithe-provision, and a gate on vouching, each grows a market on the far side of the gate.

  • Illicit Tithe-supply. Where legitimate provision is rationed, gated, or unaffordable, a shadow market fills it: smuggled green for a Taken-In cut off from land, unlicensed venting-rooms, black-market novelty (rare texts, forbidden knowledge) for a starving Long-Lived. The desperate customer is someone sliding toward the wilting, the fade, or the involuntary Cry, which makes this trade both lucrative and cruel. In the street this trade is called the Slide. The same people are called week-keepers, because the sale is rented by the week.
  • Off-book contracts. The Bound’s whole domain has an underside: contracts sworn outside the honest tables, unregistered terms, and the predatory brokers who arrange them, the pipeline that feeds the Given-Over. In the street this desk is called the Holding Desk. It sells clauses.
  • Sold vouching. Since citizenship runs on standing-for, the unvouched will buy a person who will stand for them, and a market exists in dubious character-witnesses. The one thing that can’t be faked is a Long-Lived’s memory, which is why their honest vouch is priceless and their corrupt one catastrophic. In the street this market is called the Standing Trade. It sells a witness.

The Slide sells the Tithe. The Holding Desk sells clauses. The Standing Trade sells a person who will stand.

How polities vary (economic faces)

Who pays, and who is left to pay alone, is a political choice. It runs on the same three choices as the law: how gift-devout the polity is, how many healthy Trees it has, and how far the state reaches onto the person. Three points on those choices have these economic faces:

The Warm (high theology). Treats Tithe-provision as a public good: town greenhouses, endowed venting-halls and libraries, ranging-commons held in common. The Conditioned are kept whole at civic expense because a whole populace is a safe and productive one, and because the faith says tend every gift. Its economic injustice is soft: the same provision comes with expectation. You are helped to tend your gift, and quietly obligated to spend it as the community sees fit. The subsidy can harden into a requirement.

The Watchful (governance at its extreme). The crown that writes its people down turns Tithe-need into leverage. It provides, efficiently, even generously, and it holds what it provides: the green, the outlet, the novelty, all furnished through channels the crown controls and can withhold. Its deathless-house finance is superb and centuries-deep. Its injustice is cold and precise: a population whose upkeep runs through the state is a population that cannot afford to refuse it. The crown calls that care. It is the most complete control in the world.

The Frontier (low reach). Little formal Tithe-infrastructure and few specialists. People improvise their own provision or go without. There is no guild monopoly, and no crown holding the Tithe. The Taken-In are neighbours, not curiosities. The provided-for who cannot pay their Tithe slide with no net, and untrained Struck people manage dangerous Conditions alone. At the edges the economy runs on barter and favors: cash-poor and land-rich.

A devout frontier runs commons-minded charity with almost no infrastructure to fund it. A secular Tree-rich republic has deep markets and thin welfare. A Watchful-and-pious core turns generous provision into the tightest hold in the world.

Daily Life

Most families in the Turning are ordinary. Two people marry. Some of the household carry a Condition, and some do not. A Condition is the change a person becomes. The children have not Turned yet, and will not until their tenth year. A wife born a Selkie, a husband Returned from a drowning, a Kept grandmother, three children who are simply children so far: this is the ordinary household. A Kind is what you are born, and a Selkie is one. A Condition stacks onto that Kind, and it can arrive years later. Nearly every family is mixed in that way. Mixed is the normal house, and nobody keeps a separate name for it.

Family, the whole spectrum

At the warm, mundane end (most families): a Condition is just a texture of the household, the way a trade or a temperament is. A Two-Bodied parent’s other self has a name, and the children know it. A child says, “That’s Bramble, mother’s otter,” and grows up with that other self as much a part of the family as a pet that is also a person. A Far-Voiced household is a loud one, because the Tithe means feeling must be aired daily. You do not get to sulk quietly in a home with a Far-Voiced in it, and families learn to treat the evening’s singing or weeping or laughing as ordinary weather. A Taken-In parent keeps the windows open and the rooms green. These are the small daily accommodations of living with what people have become.

In the middle, the Tithe becomes a shared family labor. Where a Condition is provided-for, keeping a family member whole is a household expense and a household duty: the green for the Taken-In, the books for the Long-Lived, the outlet for the Far-Voiced. Families organize around it the way a household organizes around a member’s chronic need, mostly without drama, sometimes with strain, and with the quiet knowledge that to let a loved one’s Tithe lapse is to watch them slide toward the harm that Tithe was holding back. A family too poor to furnish a Tithe carries a particular grief: they can see exactly what their kin needs and cannot buy it.

At the aching end sits the marriage ordinary life keeps returning to: loving someone who will not share your span of years. Chief among these is marrying a Long-Lived. Their aging simply stops; their spouse’s does not. A Long-Lived can have children and a full family, but the Condition is not reliably inherited. Most children age and die like anyone, so a Long-Lived does not build a deathless dynasty; they outlive it. They watch a spouse grow old across a marriage that stays, on one side, unchanged. They bury their children. People at those tables say the cost of the Long-Lived is measured in funerals, and it is a cost paid inside the family, at the dinner table, over decades. A Long-Lived who has done this once faces the choice their whole existence turns on: stop (live on alone, keep no more households) or begin again, marry again, love again, and outlive that family too. Both choices cost, and people live both of them. This is the setting’s deepest, quietest ache, and it lives in the most ordinary place there is: a marriage.

A few threads:

  • The threshold courtesy in the home. A Long-Lived never crosses another’s home threshold uninvited. It is held as the deepest good manners, the mark of someone safe to let in. Inside a family, this softens into something tender: to be welcomed across a Long-Lived’s own threshold, or to welcome them across yours without a thought, is a real intimacy. A door held open is how this world says you are mine and I am yours. (A door refused is felt more than a Long-Lived will ever say.)
  • The Returned come home. A person who died and Returned is warm to the touch. They are calm, deliberate, unafraid, and quietly respected, and their loved ones get them back. The chill comes if their Purpose lapses: a Returned neglecting the unfinished thing that holds them runs cold, and the living can feel the wrongness at a touch. So a Returned’s family learns a hard and intimate literacy. You can tell, by an embrace, whether the one you love is tending what holds them here. A warm embrace means they are well. A cold one means the Purpose is slipping, and the family treats that hug as an emergency.
  • Two-Bodied bloodlines are family lines. Because the Condition runs in blood (children of a Two-Bodied parent usually Turn the same animal), “wolf-folk,” “hawk-folk,” and “otter-folk” are real extended families, and the family animal, the Condition, and often the family trade descend as a single inheritance. Marrying into such a line is marrying into a shape your children will likely wear.
  • Raising a child before they Turn. Every parent lives the same open question until their child’s tenth year: what will they become, or will they be Kept? In a devout home a parent will say, “The Mother will give what the child can carry.” In an anxious one the decade is quiet dread or ambition. Parents who carry a heritable Condition (Two-Bodied especially) can half-guess; most cannot. The Leaf-Fall is therefore a civic day and the emotional hinge of a family’s life: the day you finally meet who your child has been becoming all along. In the Walking Years, a child had to be walked to Thaeloren. That is the Awakening Tree. The question before the leaf was whether the household could get there at all. A household that could walk only one child still lives in the present as two siblings, one Given and one Kept.

Medicine: miraculous, and strange to outside eyes

Two Conditions change what a sickbed and a deathbed can do. A clinic holds wonder and dread in the same room.

The Stilled are the surgeons. A stiller’s gaze arrests motion. It holds a wound from bleeding, slows a poison’s spread through the blood, keeps a patient perfectly still under the knife without holding them down. It does not petrify and does not harm; it simply stops what is moving for a beat. In a world with no anaesthetic worth the name and no way to halt a hemorrhage, this is the difference between a survivable operation and a death. The best surgeons in the world are Stilled, and a great infirmary is built around them. Sit in that operating room and watch what it is: a surgeon whose flat, patient gaze freezes the blood in your veins on command, working fast because the Grey is filling in them as they work. The Grey is the residue the Gaze leaves in the surgeon. Their Tithe is this: using the Gaze fills that Grey, and a Grey left full locks their own limb to stone. They drill and spar between patients to burn it off. The most gifted healers in the world are also the most tightly licensed and quietly feared. The same gaze that saves you on the table is the one the law watches hardest. Wonder and dread, one person, one room.

The Returned do the work that kills the living. Plague wards, mine collapses, the recovery of bodies from places the living cannot safely go, and the tending of the dead: this is Returned work, done by those death cannot take a second time until their Purpose is met. A Turning mortuary is staffed by the Returned, people who have been dead, calmly and well. A plague ward that would empty a town’s living physicians is walked by Returned who are unshaken by rot and contagion. The town respects the work. The Returned are the reason a plague does not take the doctors too, and everyone knows it. There is a real tenderness in it, too: no one speaks for the dead, or tends them, as gently as someone who has been dead.

The rest of medicine is ordinary, and ordinarily unequal. Midwives, bonesetters, herbalists, and the Answered’s craft turned to apothecary and instrument-making do the everyday care. More of the population carries a useful becoming, so that everyday care is well staffed. The great Stilled infirmaries and the deathless-endowed hospitals stand in the rich, Tree-dense core. A frontier town makes do with a travelling stiller if it is lucky. If it is not, the care is cloth, a rope, and a prayer. The deepest inequity is the Tithe-poverty already named: you can be too poor to stay whole, and the medicine that treats a lapsing Condition is the medicine the poor can least reach. The care is real. So is the queue outside it.

Living in a Turning town: city design

A city is shaped by this fact: most of its adults have an upkeep, and some of those upkeeps are dangerous or expensive to meet. The town builds for the Tithe the way it builds for water and waste: civic infrastructure, visible and load-bearing.

  • The Tree at the centre. Every town is organized around its Turning Tree. It is the civic and sacred heart, the site of the year’s most-attended day, the thing the oldest quarter grew up around. A town’s age and standing can be read in its Tree (a mature grafted Tree means an old, reach-rich town; a young Tree or none means a new or frontier settlement).
